| --- | --- | --- |
| [French](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_language) | 81% | 96% |
| [English](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/English_language) | 47% | 33% |
| [Dutch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch_language) | 22% | 33% |
| [Spanish](26825.md) | 15% | 7% |
| [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) | 12% | 10% |
| [German](11867.md) | 6% | 7% |
| [Italian](14708.md) | 6% | 5% |
| [Portuguese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Portuguese_language) | 3% | 1% |
| [Turkish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turkish_language) | 3% | 3% |
| [Romanian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Romanian_language) | 2% | - |
| [Polish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_language) | 1% | - |
| [Greek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greek_language) | 1% | - |
| [Lingala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lingala) | 1% | - |

Owing to migration and to its international role, Brussels is home to a large number of native speakers of languages other than French or Dutch. In 2024, 104 different languages were spoken by residents (72 in 2001). French was spoken "well to perfectly" by 81% (down from 96% in 2001) of the population, while for Dutch this percentage was only 22% (down from 33% in 2001), and advanced knowledge of English reached 47% (up from 33% in 2001). 29% of residents grew up in a household where neither Dutch nor French was spoken (20% in 2001), while a further 18% (9% in 2001) grew up speaking at least another foreign language (except Dutch) alongside French.[^cite_note-\:13-219] Despite the rise of English as a second language in Brussels, including as an unofficial compromise language between French and Dutch, as well as the working language for some of its international businesses and institutions, French remains the *lingua franca* and all public services are conducted exclusively in French or Dutch.[^cite_note-brusselsstudies-204]

The original dialect of Brussels (known as [Brusselian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brusselian_dialect), and also sometimes referred to as [Marollian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marollian_dialect)),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETreffers-Daller199425-56] a form of [Brabantic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brabantic) (the variant of Dutch spoken in the old [Duchy of Brabant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duchy_of_Brabant)) with a significant number of loanwords from French, still survives among a small minority of inhabitants called *Brusseleers*[^cite_note-evans-57] (or *Brusseleirs*), many of them quite bi- and multilingual.[^cite_note-winkler-220][^cite_note-FOOTNOTETreffers-Daller199425-56] The ethnic and linguistic self-identification of Brussels' inhabitants can be distinct from the French and Dutch-speaking communities. For French-speakers, it can vary from Francophone Belgian, *Bruxellois*[^cite_note-Lerond,_1980_pp._477-59] (French [demonym](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demonym) for a local inhabitant), [Walloon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Walloons) (for people who migrated from the Walloon Region); for Flemings, it is mainly either Dutch-speaking Belgian, Flemish or *Brusselaar* (Dutch demonym for an inhabitant), and often both. For *Brusseleers*, many simply consider themselves as belonging to Brussels.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETreffers-Daller199425-56]

Since 2021, the Brussels-Capital Region has recognised the Brussels regional languages, such as [Bargoens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bargoens), [Beulemans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brusselian_dialect#Varieties), [Flemish Brusselian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flemish_Brusselian_dialect) and [Marollian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marollian_dialect), as intangible cultural heritage.[^cite_note-erfgoed-221]

### Religions

Further information: [Religion in Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Religion_in_Belgium)

Religions in the Brussels-Capital Region (2016)

[^cite_note-2016religion-222]

1. [Catholicism](606848.md) (40.0%)

2. [Islam](6037917.md) (23.0%)

3. [Protestantism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestantism) (3.00%)

4. Other religions (4.00%)

5. Non-religious (30.0%)

Historically, Brussels has been predominantly [Catholic](606848.md), especially since the expulsion of [Protestants](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Protestants) in the 16th century. This is clear from the large number of historical churches in the region, particularly in the [City of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Brussels). The pre-eminent Catholic [cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral) in Brussels is the [Cathedral of St. Michael and St. Gudula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_of_St._Michael_and_St._Gudula), serving as the [co-cathedral](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Co-cathedral) of the [Archdiocese of Mechelen–Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archdiocese_of_Mechelen%E2%80%93Brussels). On the north-western side of the region, the [National Basilica of the Sacred Heart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_the_Sacred_Heart,_Brussels) is a [Minor Basilica](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minor_Basilica) and [parish church](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Parish_church), as well as one of the [largest churches by area in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_church_buildings).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStéphany200663-223] The [Church of Our Lady of Laeken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_Our_Lady_of_Laeken) holds the tombs of many members of the [Belgian royal family](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monarchy_of_Belgium), including all the former [Belgian monarchs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_monarchs), within the [Royal Crypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Crypt_\(Belgium\)).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEState2004218-224][^cite_note-225]



The [National Basilica of the Sacred Heart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_the_Sacred_Heart,_Brussels) in [Koekelberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koekelberg)

In reflection of its multicultural makeup, Brussels hosts a variety of religious communities, as well as large numbers of [atheists](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atheists) and [agnostics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agnostics). Minority faiths include Islam, [Anglicanism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anglicanism), [Eastern Orthodoxy](10186.md), [Judaism](15624.md), and Buddhism. According to a 2016 survey, approximately 40% of residents of Brussels declared themselves Catholics (12% were practising Catholics and 28% were non-practising Catholics), 30% were [non-religious](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-religious), 23% were [Muslim](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim) (19% practising, 4% non-practising), 3% were Protestants and 4% were of another religion.[^cite_note-2016religion-222]

As guaranteed by Belgian law, recognised religions and non-religious philosophical organisations (French: *organisations laïques*, Dutch: *vrijzinnige levensbeschouwelijke organisaties*)[^cite_note-berkley-226] enjoy public funding and school courses. It was once the case that every pupil in an official school from 6 to 18 years old had to choose two hours per week of compulsory religious—or non-religious-inspired morals—courses. However, in 2015, the [Belgian Constitutional Court](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_Constitutional_Court) ruled religious studies could no longer be required in the [primary](25058.md) and [secondary](554992.md) educational systems.[^cite_note-227]



The [Great Mosque of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Mosque_of_Brussels), former seat of the Islamic and Cultural Centre of Belgium

Brussels has a large concentration of [Muslims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Belgium), mostly of Moroccan, Turkish, Syrian and Guinean ancestry. The [Great Mosque of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Great_Mosque_of_Brussels), located in the [Parc du Cinquantenaire/Jubelpark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinquantenaire), is the oldest [mosque](19894.md) in Brussels and the former seat of the Islamic and Cultural Centre of Belgium.[^cite_note-228] Belgium does not collect statistics by ethnic background or religious beliefs, so exact figures are unknown.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTETorrekens2007-229] It was estimated that, in 2005, people of Muslim background living in the Brussels Region numbered 256,220 and accounted for 25.5% of the city's population, a much higher concentration than those of the other regions of Belgium.[^cite_note-BuG_100-230]

| Regions of Belgium[^cite_note-BuG_100-230] (1 January 2016) | Total population | People of Muslim origin | % of Muslims |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Belgium | 11,371,928 | 603,642 | 5.3% |
| **Brussels-Capital Region** | **1,180,531** | **212,495** | **18%** |
| *Wallonia* | *3,395,942* | *149,421* | *4.4%* |
| *Flanders* | *6,043,161* | *241,726* | *4.0%* |

## Architecture

The architecture in Brussels is diverse, and spans from the clashing combination of [Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gothic_architecture), [Baroque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baroque_architecture), and [Louis XIV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Louis_XIV_style) styles on the [Grand-Place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand-Place) to the [postmodern](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Postmodern_architecture) buildings of the [EU institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_and_the_European_Union).[^cite_note-231]



*[Manneken Pis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manneken_Pis)*, a well-known public sculpture

Very little [medieval architecture](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_architecture) is preserved in Brussels. Buildings from that period are mostly found in the historical centre (called the Îlot Sacré), [Saint Géry/Sint-Goriks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-G%C3%A9ry_Island) and Sainte-Catherine/Sint Katelijne neighbourhoods. The [Brabantine Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brabantine_Gothic) [Cathedral of St. Michael and St. Gudula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cathedral_of_St._Michael_and_St._Gudula) remains a prominent feature in the skyline of downtown Brussels. Isolated portions of the [first city walls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_walls_of_Brussels) were saved from destruction and can be seen to this day. One of the only remains of the [second walls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_walls_of_Brussels) is the [Halle Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Halle_Gate). The Grand-Place is the main attraction in the city centre and has been a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md) since 1998.[^cite_note-232] The square is dominated by the 15th-century [Flamboyant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flamboyant) [Town Hall](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Town_Hall), the [neo-Gothic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Gothic) [King's House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King's_House,_Brussels) or Bread House, and the Baroque [guildhalls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guildhall) of the former [Guilds of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Guilds_of_Brussels). *[Manneken Pis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Manneken_Pis)*, a fountain containing a small [bronze](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze) sculpture of a urinating youth, is a tourist attraction and [symbol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Symbol) of the city.[^cite_note-233]



The [Grand-Place of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand-Place), a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md)

The [neoclassical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neoclassical_architecture) style of the 18th and 19th centuries is represented in the [Royal Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Quarter)/[Coudenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Coudenberg) area, around [Brussels Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Park) and the [Place Royale/Koningsplein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_Royale,_Brussels). Examples include the [Royal Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Palace_of_Brussels), the [Church of St. James on Coudenberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_St._James_on_Coudenberg), the [Palace of the Nation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_the_Nation) (Parliament building), the [Academy Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Academy_Palace), the [Palace of Charles of Lorraine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Charles_of_Lorraine), the [Palace of the Count of Flanders](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_the_Count_of_Flanders) and the [Egmont Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Egmont_Palace). Other uniform neoclassical ensembles can be found around the [Place des Martyrs/Martelaarsplein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_des_Martyrs,_Brussels) and the [Place des Barricades/Barricadenplein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_des_Barricades). Some additional landmarks in the centre are the [Royal Saint-Hubert Galleries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Saint-Hubert_Galleries) (1847), one of the oldest covered shopping arcades in Europe, the [Congress Column](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Congress_Column) (1859), the [Bourse Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourse_Palace) (1873) and the [Palace of Justice](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palace_of_Justice,_Brussels) (1883). The latter, designed by [Joseph Poelaert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joseph_Poelaert), in [eclectic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eclecticism_in_architecture) style, is reputed to be the largest building constructed in the 19th century.[^cite_note-234]

Located outside the historical centre, in a greener environment bordering the [European Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_and_the_European_Union#European_Quarter), are the [Parc du Cinquantenaire/Jubelpark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinquantenaire) with its [memorial arcade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arcade_du_Cinquantenaire) and nearby museums, and in [Laeken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laeken), the [Royal Palace of Laeken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Palace_of_Laeken) and the [Royal Domain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Domain_of_Laeken) with its large [greenhouses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Greenhouses_of_Laeken), as well as the [Museums of the Far East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Museums_of_the_Far_East).

Also particularly striking are the buildings in the [Art Nouveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Nouveau_in_Brussels) style, most famously by the Belgian architects [Victor Horta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Horta), [Paul Hankar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Hankar) and [Henry Van de Velde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Henry_Van_de_Velde).[^cite_note-235][^cite_note-236] Some of Brussels' municipalities, such as [Schaerbeek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schaerbeek), [Etterbeek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etterbeek), [Ixelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ixelles), and [Saint-Gilles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Gilles,_Belgium), were developed during the heyday of Art Nouveau and have many buildings in that style. The [Major Town Houses of the Architect Victor Horta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Major_Town_Houses_of_the_Architect_Victor_Horta)—[Hôtel Tassel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B4tel_Tassel) (1893), [Hôtel van Eetvelde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B4tel_van_Eetvelde) (1898), [Hôtel Solvay](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H%C3%B4tel_Solvay) (1900) and the [Horta Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Horta_Museum) (1901)—have been listed as a UNESCO World Heritage Site since 2000.[^cite_note-\:6-91] Another example of Brussels' Art Nouveau is the [Stoclet Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoclet_Palace) (1911), by the Viennese architect [Josef Hoffmann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Josef_Hoffmann), designated a World Heritage Site by UNESCO in June 2009.[^cite_note-237]
