[Flagey Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flagey_Building) in [Ixelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ixelles)

[Art Deco](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Deco_in_Brussels) structures in Brussels include the Résidence Palace (1927) (now part of the [Europa building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Europa_building)), the [Centre for Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_for_Fine_Arts) (1928), the [Villa Empain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Villa_Empain) (1934), the [Town Hall of Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_Town_Hall) (1938), and the [Flagey Building](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flagey_Building) (also known as the *Radio House*) on the [Place Eugène Flagey/Eugène Flageyplein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_Eug%C3%A8ne_Flagey) (1938) in Ixelles. Some religious buildings from the [interwar era](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Interwar_era) were also constructed in that style, such as the [Church of St. John the Baptist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_St._John_the_Baptist,_Molenbeek) (1932) in Molenbeek and the [Church of St. Augustine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Church_of_St._Augustine,_Forest) (1935) in Forest. Completed only in 1969, and combining Art Deco with [neo-Byzantine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neo-Byzantine) elements, the [Basilica of the Sacred Heart](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basilica_of_the_Sacred_Heart,_Brussels) in [Koekelberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koekelberg) is one of the [largest churches by area in the world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_church_buildings_in_the_world), and its [cupola](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cupola) provides a panoramic view of Brussels and its outskirts. Another example are the exhibition halls of the Centenary Palace, built for the [1935 World's Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_International_Exposition_\(1935\)) on the [Heysel/Heizel Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heysel_Plateau) in northern Brussels, home to the Brussels Exhibition Centre ([Brussels Expo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Expo)).[^cite_note-238]



The [Atomium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomium)

The [Atomium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atomium) is a symbolic 103 m-tall (338 ft) [modernist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_architecture) structure, located on the Heysel Plateau, which was originally built for the 1958 World's Fair ([Expo 58](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Expo_58)). It consists of nine steel spheres connected by tubes, and forms a model of an [iron](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iron) [crystal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crystal) (specifically, a [unit cell](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unit_cell)), magnified 165 billion times. The architect [André Waterkeyn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andr%C3%A9_Waterkeyn) devoted the building to science. It is now considered a [landmark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Landmark) of Brussels.[^cite_note-239][^cite_note-240] Next to the Atomium, is [Mini-Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mini-Europe) [miniature park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Miniature_park), with 1\:25 scale [maquettes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maquette) of famous buildings from across Europe.

Since the second half of the 20th century, modern office towers have been built in Brussels ([Madou Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madou_Tower), [Rogier Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rogier_Tower), [Proximus Towers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proximus_Towers), [Finance Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Finance_Tower), the [World Trade Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Center_\(Brussels\)), among others). There are some thirty towers, mostly concentrated in the city's [main business district](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_business_district): the [Northern Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Quarter_\(Brussels\)) (also called *Little Manhattan*), near [Brussels-North railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels-North_railway_station). The [South Tower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Tower_\(Brussels\)), standing adjacent to [Brussels-South railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels-South_railway_station), is the [tallest building in Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_tallest_structures_in_Belgium), at 148 m (486 ft). Along the [North–South connection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North%E2%80%93South_connection), is the State Administrative Centre, an administrative complex in the [International Style](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Style). The postmodern buildings of the [Espace Léopold](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Espace_L%C3%A9opold) complete the picture.

The city's embrace of modern architecture translated into an ambivalent approach towards historic preservation, leading to the destruction of notable architectural landmarks, most famously the [Maison du Peuple/Volkshuis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maison_du_Peuple,_Brussels) by Victor Horta, a process known as [Brusselisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brusselisation).[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEState200451–52-100][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEStubbsMakaš2011121-101]

## Culture

Further information: [Culture of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Brussels) and [Culture of Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Culture_of_Belgium)

### Visual arts and museums



[Cinquantenaire/Jubelpark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinquantenaire) [memorial arcade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cinquantenaire_Arcade) and museums

Brussels [contains over 80 museums](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_museums_in_Brussels).[^cite_note-241] The [Royal Museums of Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Museums_of_Fine_Arts_of_Belgium) has an extensive collection of various painters, such as [Flemish old masters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flemish_painting) like [Bruegel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pieter_Bruegel_the_Elder), [Rogier van der Weyden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rogier_van_der_Weyden), [Robert Campin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Robert_Campin), [Anthony van Dyck](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anthony_van_Dyck), [Jacob Jordaens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jacob_Jordaens), and [Peter Paul Rubens](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peter_Paul_Rubens). The [Magritte Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magritte_Museum) houses the world's largest collection of works by the [surrealist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Surrealist) artist [René Magritte](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Magritte). Museums dedicated to the national history of Belgium include the [BELvue Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BELvue_Museum), the [Royal Museums of Art and History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Museums_of_Art_and_History), and the [Royal Museum of the Armed Forces and Military History](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Museum_of_the_Armed_Forces_and_Military_History). The [Musical Instruments Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Musical_Instruments_Museum,_Brussels) (MIM), housed in the [Old England](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Old_England_\(department_store\)) building, is part of the Royal Museums of Art and History, and is internationally renowned for its collection of over 8,000 instruments.

The Brussels Museums Council is an independent body for all the museums in the Brussels-Capital Region, covering around 100 federal, private, municipal, and community museums.[^cite_note-242] It promotes member museums through the Brussels Card (giving access to public transport and 30 of the 100 museums), the Brussels Museums Nocturnes (every Thursday from 5 p.m. to 10 p.m. from mid-September to mid-December) and Museum Night Fever (an event for and by young people on a Saturday night in late February or early March).[^cite_note-243] Moreover, on the first Wednesday or Sunday of every month, free entry is granted to many of Brussels' museums.[^cite_note-244]

Brussels has had a distinguished art scene for many years. The Belgian surrealists René Magritte and [Paul Delvaux](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paul_Delvaux), for instance, studied and lived there, as did the [avant-garde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avant-garde) dramatist [Michel de Ghelderode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michel_de_Ghelderode). The city was also home of the [impressionist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Impressionist) painter [Anna Boch](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anna_Boch) from the artists' group *[Les XX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Les_XX)*, and includes other Belgian painters such as [Léon Spilliaert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/L%C3%A9on_Spilliaert). Brussels is also a capital of the [comic strip](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Comic_strip);[^cite_note-Comic-3] some treasured Belgian characters are *[Tintin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Adventures_of_Tintin)*, *[Lucky Luke](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lucky_Luke)*, *[The Smurfs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Smurfs)*, *[Spirou](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spirou_et_Fantasio)*, *[Gaston](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gaston_\(comics\))*, *[Marsupilami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marsupilami)*, *[Blake and Mortimer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blake_and_Mortimer)*, *[Boule et Bill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boule_et_Bill)* and *[Cubitus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cubitus)* (see [Belgian comics](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_comics)). Throughout the city, walls are painted with large motifs of comic book characters; these [murals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mural) taken together are known as [Brussels' Comic Book Route](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels'_Comic_Book_Route).[^cite_note-\:1-51] Also, the interiors of some [Metro stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Metro) are designed by artists. The [Belgian Comic Strip Center](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_Comic_Strip_Center) combines two artistic leitmotifs of Brussels, being a museum devoted to Belgian comic strips, housed in the former *Magasins Waucquez* textile department store, designed by [Victor Horta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Victor_Horta) in the [Art Nouveau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Art_Nouveau_in_Brussels) style. In addition, [street art](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Street_art) is changing the landscape of this multicultural city.[^cite_note-245]

### Performing arts venues and festivals



[Royal Theatre of La Monnaie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Monnaie)

Brussels is well known for its [performing arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Performing_arts) scene, with the [Royal Theatre of La Monnaie](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Monnaie), the [Royal Park Theatre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Park_Theatre), the [Théâtre Royal des Galeries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Th%C3%A9%C3%A2tre_Royal_des_Galeries), and the [Kaaitheater](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kaaitheater) among the most notable institutions.

The [Kunstenfestivaldesarts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kunstenfestivaldesarts), an international performing arts festival, is organised every year in May. Its main hub is the Kaaitheater, but performances and artworks are also hosted in around 30 venues throughout the city.[^cite_note-246][^cite_note-247]

The [King Baudouin Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Baudouin_Stadium) is a concert and competition facility with a 50,000 seat capacity, the largest in Belgium. The site was formerly occupied by the [Heysel Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heysel_Stadium). The [Centre for Fine Arts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Centre_for_Fine_Arts) (often referred to as BOZAR in French or PSK in Dutch), a multi-purpose centre for theatre, cinema, music, literature, and art exhibitions, is home to the [National Orchestra of Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Orchestra_of_Belgium) and to the annual [Queen Elisabeth Competition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Queen_Elisabeth_Competition) for [classical](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_music) singers and instrumentalists, one of the most challenging and prestigious competitions of the kind. Studio 4 in [Le Flagey](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Flagey) cultural centre hosts the [Brussels Philharmonic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Philharmonic).[^cite_note-248][^cite_note-249]

Other concert venues include [Forest National/Vorst Nationaal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest_National), the [Ancienne Belgique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ancienne_Belgique), the [Cirque Royal/Koninklijk Circus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cirque_Royal), the [Botanique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Botanique) and [Palais 12/Paleis 12](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palais_12). Furthermore, the [Jazz Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz_Station) in [Saint-Josse-ten-Noode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Josse-ten-Noode) is a museum and archive on [jazz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jazz), and a venue for jazz concerts.[^cite_note-250]

### Other cultural events and festivals



[Brussels Summer Festival](https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels%20Summer%20Festival) (BSF)

Many events are organised or hosted in Brussels throughout the year. In addition, many festivals animate the Brussels scene.

The [Iris Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iris_Festival) is the official festival of the Brussels-Capital Region and is held annually in spring.[^cite_note-251] The International Fantastic Film Festival of Brussels (BIFFF) is organised during the Easter holidays[^cite_note-252] and the [Magritte Awards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Magritte_Award) in February. The [Festival of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Festival_of_Europe), an open day and activities in and around the [European Union](9317.md)'s institutions, is held on 9 May. On [Belgian National Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_National_Day), on 21 July, a military parade and celebrations take place on the Place des Palais/Paleizenplein and in [Brussels Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Park), ending with a fireworks display in the evening.

Some summer festivities include [Couleur Café Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Couleur_Caf%C3%A9_Festival), a festival of [world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_music) and [urban music](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Urban_music), around the end of June or early July, the [Brussels Summer Festival](https://fr.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels%20Summer%20Festival) (BSF), a music festival in August,[^cite_note-253] the [Midi Fair](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midi_Fair), the most important yearly fair in Brussels, lasting more than a month, in July and August,[^cite_note-254] and Brussels Beach, when the banks of the [canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels%E2%80%93Charleroi_Canal) are turned into a temporary urban beach.[^cite_note-255] Other biennial events are the [Zinneke Parade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zinneke_Parade), a colourful, multicultural parade through the city, which has been held since 2000 in May, as well as the popular [Flower Carpet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flower_Carpet_\(Brussels\)) at the [Grand-Place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand-Place) in August. [Heritage Days](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Heritage_Days) are organised on the third weekend of September (sometimes coinciding with the car-free day) and are a good opportunity to discover the wealth of buildings, institutions and real estate in Brussels. The "Winter Wonders" animate the heart of Brussels in December; these winter activities were launched in Brussels in 2001.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEState2004108-256][^cite_note-257]

### Folklore

Further information: [Folklore of Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Folklore_of_Belgium)



[Meyboom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meyboom) giants in Brussels, a [UNESCO](21786641.md) [Intangible Cultural Heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intangible_Cultural_Heritage)

Brussels' identity owes much to its rich folklore and traditions, among the liveliest in the country.[^cite_note-258][^cite_note-259]

The [Ommegang](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ommegang_of_Brussels), a [medieval pageant](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Medieval_pageant) and folkloric costumed procession, commemorating the [Joyous Entry](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joyous_Entry) of [Emperor Charles V](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emperor_Charles_V) and his son, [Philip II](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Philip_II_of_Spain), in the city in 1549, takes place every year in July. The colourful parade includes floats, traditional processional giants, such as Saint [Michael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michael_\(archangel\)) and Saint [Gudula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gudula), and scores of folkloric groups, either on foot or on horseback, dressed in medieval garb. The parade concludes with a large spectacle at the [Grand-Place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand-Place). Since 2019, it has been recognised as a [Masterpiece of the Oral and Intangible Heritage of Humanity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Masterpiece_of_the_Oral_and_Intangible_Heritage_of_Humanity) by [UNESCO](21786641.md).[^cite_note-260]

The [Meyboom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meyboom), an even-older folk tradition of Brussels (1308), celebrating the "[May tree](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maypole)"—in fact, a corruption of the Dutch word, meaning *tree of joy*—takes place paradoxically on 9 August. After parading a young [beech](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beech) in the city, it is planted in a joyful spirit with lots of music, *Brusseleir* songs, and processional giants. It has also been recognised as an expression of [intangible cultural heritage](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intangible_cultural_heritage) by UNESCO, as part of the bi-national inscription "[Processional giants and dragons in Belgium and France](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Processional_giants_and_dragons_in_Belgium_and_France)".[^cite_note-261][^cite_note-262] The celebration is reminiscent of the town's long-standing (folkloric) feud with [Leuven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leuven), which dates back to the [Middle Ages](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_Ages).

[Saint Verhaegen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint_Verhaegen) (often shortened to *St V*), a folkloric student procession, celebrating the anniversary of the founding of the [Université libre de Bruxelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universit%C3%A9_libre_de_Bruxelles) (ULB) and the [Vrije Universiteit Brussel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vrije_Universiteit_Brussel) (VUB), is held on 20 November. Since 2019, it has also been listed as intangible cultural heritage of the Brussels-Capital Region.[^cite_note-263][^cite_note-264][^cite_note-265]

Another good introduction to the *Brusseleir* [local dialect](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brusselian_dialect) and way of life can be obtained at the [Royal Theatre Toone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Theatre_Toone), a folkloric theatre of [marionettes](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marionette), located a stone's throw away from the Grand-Place.[^cite_note-266] Finally, two folkloric plays, *[Le Mariage de mademoiselle Beulemans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Mariage_de_mademoiselle_Beulemans)* by Frantz Fonson and Fernand Wicheler, and *[Bossemans et Coppenolle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bossemans_et_Coppenolle)* by Joris d'Hanswyck and Paul Van Stalle, are still the subject of regular revivals.[^cite_note-267]

## Cuisine

Main article: [Cuisine of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuisine_of_Brussels)



Brussels is known for its [local waffles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Waffle#Brussels).

Brussels is well known for its local [waffle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_waffle), its [chocolate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_chocolate), its [French fries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_fries) and its numerous types of [beers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beer_in_Belgium). The [Brussels sprout](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_sprout), which has long been popular in Brussels, and may have originated there, is also named after the city.[^cite_note-268]

Owing to Brussels' cosmopolitan population, almost every national cuisine in the world can be found there. The gastronomic offer includes approximately 1,800 [restaurants](26437.md) (including three 2-starred and ten 1-starred [Michelin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Michelin_Guide) restaurants),[^cite_note-269] and a number of bars. In addition to the traditional restaurants, there are many [cafés](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caf%C3%A9s), [bistros](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bistro) and the usual range of international [fast food](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fast_food_restaurant) chains. The cafés are similar to bars, and offer beer and light dishes; [coffee houses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Coffee_houses) are called *salons de thé* (literally "tea salons"). Also widespread are [brasseries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brasserie), which usually offer a variety of beers and typical national dishes.

[Belgian cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_cuisine) is known among connoisseurs as one of the best in Europe. It is characterised by the combination of [French cuisine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French_cuisine) with the more hearty Flemish fare. Notable specialities include [Brussels waffles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_waffle) (gaufres) and [mussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mussels) (usually as [*moules-frites*](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moules_frites), served with fries). The city is a stronghold of [chocolate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_chocolate) and [praline](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Praline_\(Belgian_chocolate\)) manufacturers with renowned companies like [Côte d'Or](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C%C3%B4te_d'Or_\(chocolate\)), [Neuhaus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chocolatier_Neuhaus), [Leonidas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonidas_\(chocolate_maker\)) and [Godiva](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Godiva_Chocolatier). Pralines were first introduced in 1912 by Jean Neuhaus II, a Belgian [chocolatier](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Chocolatier) of Swiss origin, in the [Royal Saint-Hubert Galleries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Saint-Hubert_Galleries).[^cite_note-270] Numerous [friteries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Friteries) are spread throughout the city, and in tourist areas, fresh hot waffles are also sold on the street.

As well as other [Belgian beers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Beer_in_Belgium), the [spontaneously fermented](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Spontaneously_fermented) [lambic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lambic) style, brewed in and around Brussels, is widely available there and in the nearby [Senne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Senne_\(river\)) valley where the wild [yeasts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yeast) that ferment it have their origin.[^cite_note-Jackson1988-271] [Kriek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kriek), a cherry lambic, is available in almost every bar or restaurant in Brussels.

Brussels is known as the birthplace of the [Belgian endive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_endive). The technique for growing [blanched](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blanching_\(horticulture\)) endives was accidentally discovered in the 1850s at the [Botanical Garden of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Botanical_Garden_of_Brussels) in [Saint-Josse-ten-Noode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Josse-ten-Noode).[^cite_note-272]

## Shopping



[Flea market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flea_market) on the [Place du Jeu de Balle/Vossenplein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_du_Jeu_de_Balle)

Famous [shopping](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shopping) areas in Brussels include the pedestrian-only [Rue Neuve/Nieuwstraat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rue_Neuve_\(Brussels\)), the second busiest shopping street in Belgium (after the [Meir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meir,_Antwerp), in [Antwerp](32149462.md)) with a weekly average of 230,000 visitors,[^cite_note-273][^cite_note-274] home to popular international chains ([H\&M](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/H\&M), [C\&A](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/C\&A), [Zara](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zara_\(retailer\)), [Primark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primark)), as well as the City 2 and Anspach galleries.[^cite_note-\:3-275] The [Royal Saint-Hubert Galleries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Saint-Hubert_Galleries) hold a variety of luxury shops and some six million people stroll through them each year.[^cite_note-276] The neighbourhood around the Rue Antoine Dansaert/Antoine Dansaertstraat has also become, in recent years, a focal point for fashion and design.[^cite_note-277][^cite_note-278]

In Ixelles, the Avenue de la Toison d'Or/Gulden-Vlieslaan and the [Namur Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Namur_Gate) area have luxury shops, fast food restaurants and entertainment venues. Another shopping area is the Chaussée d'Ixelles/Elsenesteenweg, in the mainly-Congolese *[Matongé](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ixelles#Matong%C3%A9)* district. The nearby [Avenue Louise/Louizalaan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Avenue_Louise/Louizalaan) is lined with high-end fashion stores and boutiques, making it one of the most expensive streets in Belgium.[^cite_note-279]

There are shopping centres outside the inner ring: Basilix, Woluwe Shopping Center, Westland Shopping Center, and Docks Bruxsel, which opened in October 2017.[^cite_note-\:3-275] In addition, Brussels ranks as one of Europe's best capital cities for [flea market](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flea_market) shopping. The *Old Market*, on the [Place du Jeu de Balle/Vossenplein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_du_Jeu_de_Balle), in the [Marolles/Marollen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marollen) neighbourhood, is particularly renowned.[^cite_note-280] The nearby [Sablon/Zavel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sablon_\(Brussels\)) area is home to many of Brussels' [antique](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antique) dealers.[^cite_note-281] The *Midi Market* around [Brussels-South station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels-South_station) and the Boulevard du Midi/Zuidlaan is reputed to be one of the largest markets in Europe.[^cite_note-282]

## Sports

Further information: [Sport in Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sport_in_Belgium)



[20 km of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20_km_of_Brussels)

Sport in Brussels is under the responsibility of the [Communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communities,_regions_and_language_areas_of_Belgium). The *Administration de l'Éducation Physique et du Sport* ([ADEPS](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ADEPS)) is responsible for recognising the various French-speaking sports federations and also runs three sports centres in the Brussels-Capital Region.[^cite_note-283] Its Dutch-speaking counterpart is *Sport Vlaanderen* (formerly called [BLOSO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bloso)).[^cite_note-284]

The [King Baudouin Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Baudouin_Stadium) (formerly the Heysel Stadium) is the largest in the country and home to the national teams in [football](679716.md) and [rugby union](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgium_national_rugby_union_team).[^cite_note-285] It hosted the final of the [1972 UEFA European Football Championship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1972_UEFA_European_Football_Championship), and the opening game of [the 2000 edition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euro_2000). Several European club finals have been held at the ground, including the [1985 European Cup Final](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1985_European_Cup_Final), which saw 39 deaths due to hooliganism and structural collapse.[^cite_note-286] The King Baudouin Stadium is also home of the annual [Memorial Van Damme](1111662.md) athletics event, Belgium's foremost [track and field](194904.md) competition, which is part of the [Diamond League](21778161.md). Other important athletics events are the [Brussels Marathon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Marathon)[^cite_note-287] and the [20 km of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/20_km_of_Brussels), an annual run with 30,000 participants.[^cite_note-288]

### Football



[RSC Anderlecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RSC_Anderlecht) fans at the [Constant Vanden Stock Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constant_Vanden_Stock_Stadium)

[RSC Anderlecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RSC_Anderlecht), based in the [Constant Vanden Stock Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Constant_Vanden_Stock_Stadium) in [Anderlecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anderlecht), is the most successful Belgian football club in the [Belgian Pro League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_Pro_League), with 34 titles.[^cite_note-289] It has also won the most major European tournaments for a Belgian side, with 6 European titles. Brussels is also home to [Union Saint-Gilloise](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Union_Saint-Gilloise), the most successful Belgian club before World War II, with 12 titles.[^cite_note-290] The club was founded in [Saint-Gilles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Gilles,_Belgium) but is based in nearby [Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest,_Belgium), and plays in the Belgian Pro League. [Racing White Daring Molenbeek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RWDM47), based in [Molenbeek-Saint-Jean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molenbeek-Saint-Jean), and often referred to as RWDM, is a very popular football club that, since 2023, is back playing in the Belgian Pro League.[^cite_note-291][^cite_note-292]

Other Brussels clubs that played in the national series over the years were [Royal White Star Bruxelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_White_Star_Bruxelles), Ixelles SC, [Crossing Club de Schaerbeek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crossing_Club_de_Schaerbeek) (born from a merger between RCS de Schaerbeek and Crossing Club Molenbeek), Scup Jette, RUS de Laeken, Racing Jet de Bruxelles, AS Auderghem, KV Wosjot Woluwe and FC Ganshoren.

### Cycling

Brussels is home to notable [cycling races](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cycling_race). The city is the arrival location of the [Brussels Cycling Classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Cycling_Classic), formerly known as Paris–Brussels, which is one of the oldest [semi classic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classic_cycle_races) [bicycle races](1755460.md) on the international calendar.[^cite_note-293] From World War I until the early 1970s, the [Six Days of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Six_Days_of_Brussels) was organised regularly. In the last decades of the 20th century, the [Grand Prix Eddy Merckx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Prix_Eddy_Merckx) was also held in Brussels.

## Economy

Further information: [Economy of Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Belgium)



The [Bourse Palace](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bourse_Palace), the former [Brussels Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Stock_Exchange) (BSE) building

Serving as the centre of administration for Belgium and Europe, Brussels' economy is largely [service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tertiary_sector_of_the_economy)-oriented. It is dominated by regional and world headquarters of [multinationals](214491.md), by European institutions, by various local and federal administrations, and by related services companies, though it does have a number of notable [craft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Craft) industries, such as the [Cantillon Brewery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cantillon_Brewery), a [lambic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lambic) brewery founded in 1900.[^cite_note-294]



Brussels' [Northern Quarter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Northern_Quarter_\(Brussels\)) business district

Brussels has a robust economy. The region contributes to one fifth of Belgium's [GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP), and its 550,000 jobs account for 17.7% of Belgium's employment.[^cite_note-295] Its [GDP per capita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/GDP_per_capita) is nearly double that of Belgium as a whole,[^cite_note-\:4-29] and it has the highest GDP per capita of any [NUTS 1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nomenclature_of_Territorial_Units_for_Statistics) region in the EU, at \~\$80,000 in 2016.[^cite_note-296] However, the GDP is boosted by a massive inflow of [commuters](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Commuters) from neighbouring regions; over half of those who work in Brussels live in Flanders or Wallonia, with 230,000 and 130,000 commuters per day respectively. Conversely, only 16.0% of Brussels residents work outside the region (68,827 (68.5%) of them in Flanders and 21,035 (31.5%) in Wallonia).[^cite_note-297] Not all of the wealth generated in Brussels remains in the region, and as of December 2013, the unemployment among its residents is 20.4%.[^cite_note-298]

There are approximately 50,000 [businesses](39206.md) in Brussels, of which around 2,200 are foreign. This number is constantly increasing and can well explain the role of Brussels in Europe. The city's [infrastructure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrastructure) is very favourable in terms of starting up a new business. House prices have also increased in recent years, especially with the increase of young [professionals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Professional) settling down in Brussels, making it the most expensive city to live in Belgium.[^cite_note-299] In addition, Brussels holds more than 1,000 business conferences annually, making it the ninth most popular conference city in Europe.[^cite_note-300]

Brussels is rated as the 34th most important financial centre in the world as of 2020, according to the [Global Financial Centres Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Financial_Centres_Index). The [Brussels Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Stock_Exchange), abbreviated to BSE and now called Euronext Brussels, is part of the European stock exchange [Euronext](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euronext), along with [Paris Bourse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paris_Bourse), [Lisbon Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lisbon_Stock_Exchange) and [Amsterdam Stock Exchange](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Amsterdam_Stock_Exchange). Its benchmark [stock market index](23408957.md) is the [BEL20](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BEL20).

## Media

Further information: [Belgian media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_media)

Brussels is a centre of both media and communications in Belgium, with many Belgian television stations, radio stations, newspapers and telephone companies having their headquarters in the region. The French-language public broadcaster [RTBF](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RTBF), the Dutch-language public broadcaster [VRT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vlaamse_Radio-_en_Televisieomroeporganisatie), the two regional channels BX1 (formerly Télé Bruxelles)[^cite_note-301] and [Bruzz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bruzz) (formerly TV Brussel),[^cite_note-302] the encrypted [BeTV](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BeTV_\(Belgium\)) channel and private channels [RTL-TVI](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/RTL-TVI) and [VTM](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/VTM_\(TV_channel\)) are headquartered in Brussels. Some national newspapers such as *[Le Soir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Le_Soir)*, *[La Libre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/La_Libre)*, *[De Morgen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_Morgen)* and the news agency [Belga](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belga_\(news_agency\)) are based in or around Brussels. The Belgian postal company [bpost](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bpost), as well as the telecommunication companies and mobile operators [Proximus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Proximus), [Orange Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orange_Belgium) and [Telenet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Telenet_\(Belgium\)) are all located there.

As English is spoken widely,[^cite_note-BruS13EN-46][^cite_note-\:7-47] several English media organisations operate in Brussels. The most popular of these are the English-language daily news media platform and bi-monthly magazine *[The Brussels Times](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Brussels_Times)* and the website *[The Bulletin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Bulletin_\(Brussels_weekly\))*. The multilingual [pan-European](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pan-European_identity) news channel [Euronews](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Euronews) also maintains an office in Brussels.[^cite_note-303]

## Education

Further information: [Education in Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Education_in_Belgium)

### Tertiary education



[Solbosch/Solbos](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neighbourhoods_in_Brussels#Solbosch/Solbos) campus of the [Université libre de Bruxelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universit%C3%A9_libre_de_Bruxelles) (ULB)

There are several [universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universities) in Brussels. Except for the [Royal Military Academy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Military_Academy_\(Belgium\)), a federal military college established in 1834,[^cite_note-304] all universities in Brussels are private and autonomous. The Royal Military Academy is also the only Belgian university organised on the [boarding school](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boarding_school) model.[^cite_note-305]

The [Université libre de Bruxelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universit%C3%A9_libre_de_Bruxelles) (ULB), a French-speaking university, with about 20,000 students, has three campuses in the city,[^cite_note-306] and the [Vrije Universiteit Brussel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vrije_Universiteit_Brussel) (VUB), its [Dutch-speaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch-speaking) sister university, has about 10,000 students.[^cite_note-307] Both universities originate from a single ancestor university, founded in 1834, namely the [Free University of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Free_University_of_Brussels_\(1834%E2%80%931969\)), which was split in 1970, at about the same time the Flemish and French Communities gained legislative power over the organisation of higher education.[^cite_note-308]

[Saint-Louis University, Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Louis_University,_Brussels) (also known as UCLouvain Saint-Louis – Bruxelles) was founded in 1858 and is specialised in social and [human sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_science), with 4,000 students, and located on two campuses in the [City of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Brussels) and [Ixelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ixelles).[^cite_note-309] From September 2018 on, the university uses the name *UCLouvain*, together with the [Catholic University of Louvain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universit%C3%A9_catholique_de_Louvain), in the context of a merger between both universities.[^cite_note-310]

Still other universities have campuses in Brussels, such as the French-speaking Catholic University of Louvain (UCLouvain), which has 10,000 students in the city with its medical faculties at [UCLouvain Bruxelles Woluwe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UCLouvain_Bruxelles_Woluwe) since 1973,[^cite_note-311] in addition to its [Faculty of Architecture, Architectural Engineering and Urban Planning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faculty_of_Architecture,_Architectural_Engineering_and_Urban_Planning)[^cite_note-312] and UCLouvain's Dutch-speaking sister [Katholieke Universiteit Leuven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Katholieke_Universiteit_Leuven) (KU Leuven)[^cite_note-313] (offering bachelor's and master's degrees in economics & business, law, arts, and architecture; 4,400 students). In addition, the University of Kent's [Brussels School of International Studies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_School_of_International_Studies) is a specialised postgraduate school offering advanced international studies.

Also a dozen of university colleges are located in Brussels, including two drama schools, founded in 1832: the French-speaking [Conservatoire Royal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Conservatoire_royal_de_Bruxelles) and its [Dutch-speaking](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dutch-speaking) equivalent, the [Koninklijk Conservatorium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koninklijk_Conservatorium_\(Brussel\)).[^cite_note-314][^cite_note-315]

### Primary and secondary education

Most of Brussels pupils between the ages of 3 and 18 go to schools organised by the [French-speaking Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/French-speaking_Community) or the [Flemish Community](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flemish_Community), with close to 80% going to French-speaking schools, and roughly 20% to Dutch-speaking schools. Due to the post-war international presence in the city, there are also a number of international schools, including the [International School of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_School_of_Brussels), with 1,450 pupils, between the ages of 2+1⁄2 and 18,[^cite_note-316] the [British School of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/British_School_of_Brussels), and the four [European Schools](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_School), which provide free education for the children of those working in the [EU institutions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/EU_institutions). The combined student population of the four European Schools in Brussels is around 10,000.[^cite_note-317]

### Libraries

Main article: [List of libraries in Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_libraries_in_Belgium)



[Royal Library of Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Library_of_Belgium) (KBR)

Brussels has a number of public or private-owned [libraries](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Libraries) on its territory.[^cite_note-318] Most public libraries in Brussels fall under the competence of the Communities and are usually separated between French-speaking and Dutch-speaking institutions, although some are mixed.

The [Royal Library of Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Library_of_Belgium) (KBR) is the [national library](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_library) of Belgium and one of the most prestigious libraries in the world. It owns several collections of historical importance, like the [Fétis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/F%C3%A9tis) archives, and is the depository for all books ever published in Belgium or abroad by Belgian authors. It is located on the [Mont des Arts/Kunstberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mont_des_Arts) in central Brussels, near the [Central Station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Central_Station).[^cite_note-319]

There are several academic libraries and archives in Brussels. The libraries of the [Université libre de Bruxelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universit%C3%A9_libre_de_Bruxelles) (ULB) and the [Vrije Universiteit Brussel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vrije_Universiteit_Brussel) (VUB) constitute the largest ensemble of university libraries in the city. In addition to the Solbosch location, there are branches in [La Plaine/Het Plein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neighbourhoods_in_Brussels#La_Plaine/Het_Plein) and [Erasme/Erasmus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erasmus_metro_station).[^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321] Other academic libraries include those of [Saint-Louis University, Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Louis_University,_Brussels)[^cite_note-322] and the [Catholic University of Louvain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universit%C3%A9_catholique_de_Louvain) (UCLouvain).[^cite_note-323]

### Science and technology

Main article: [Science and technology in Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_and_technology_in_Brussels)



[Planetarium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetarium_\(Belgium\)) of the [Royal Observatory of Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Observatory_of_Belgium)

Science and technology in Brussels is well-developed with the presence of several [universities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_universities_in_Belgium) and research institutes. The Brussels-Capital Region is home to several national science and technology institutes including the [National Fund for Scientific Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Fund_for_Scientific_Research) (NFSR), the Institute for the Encouragement of Scientific Research and Innovation of Brussels (ISRIB), the [Royal Academies for Science and the Arts of Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Academies_for_Science_and_the_Arts_of_Belgium) (RASAB) and the [Belgian Academy Council of Applied Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_Academy_Council_of_Applied_Sciences) (BACAS). Several science parks associated with the universities are also spread over the region.

The [Royal Belgian Institute of Natural Sciences](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Belgian_Institute_of_Natural_Sciences), located in [Leopold Park](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leopold_Park), houses the world's largest hall completely dedicated to [dinosaurs](8311.md), with its collection of 30 [fossilised](10958.md) *[Iguanodon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iguanodon)* skeletons.[^cite_note-324] In addition, the [Planetarium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Planetarium_\(Belgium\)) of the [Royal Observatory of Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Observatory_of_Belgium) (part of the institutions of the [Belgian Federal Science Policy Office](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Belgian_Federal_Science_Policy_Office)), on the [Heysel Plateau](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Heysel_Plateau) in [Laeken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laeken), is one of the largest in Europe.[^cite_note-325]

### Healthcare

Main article: [List of hospitals in Belgium § Brussels-Capital Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_hospitals_in_Belgium#Brussels-Capital_Region)



[Erasmus Hospital](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erasmus_Hospital) in [Anderlecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anderlecht)

Brussels is home to a thriving [pharmaceutical](560876.md) and [health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care) industry, which includes pioneering [biotechnology](4502.md) research. The health sector employs 70,000 employees in 30,000 companies. There are 3,000 life sciences researchers in the city and two large [science parks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Science_park): Da Vinci Research Park and Erasmus Research Park. There are five [university hospitals](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/University_hospitals), a [military hospital](2359260.md) and more than 40 general hospitals and specialist clinics.[^cite_note-326]

Due to [its bilingual nature](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Languages_of_Belgium), hospitals in the Brussels-Capital Region can be either monolingual French, monolingual Dutch, or bilingual, depending on their nature. University hospitals belong to one of the two [linguistic communities](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communities,_regions_and_language_areas_of_Belgium) and are thus monolingual French or Dutch by law. Other hospitals managed by a public authority must be legally bilingual. Private hospitals are legally not bound to either language, but most cater to both. However, all hospital emergency services in the Capital Region (whether part of a public or private hospital) are required to be bilingual, since patients transported by emergency [ambulance](146317.md) cannot choose the hospital they will be brought to.[^cite_note-327]

## Transport

Main article: [Transport in Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Brussels)

Brussels has an extensive network of both private or public transportation means. Public transportation includes [Brussels buses](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_buses), [trams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_trams), and [metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Metro) (all three operated by the [Brussels Intercommunal Transport Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Intercommunal_Transport_Company) (STIB/MIVB)), as well as a set of railway lines (operated by [Infrabel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Infrabel)) and railway stations served by public trains (operated by the [National Railway Company of Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Railway_Company_of_Belgium) (SNCB/NMBS)). Air transport is available via one of the city's two airports ([Brussels Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Airport) and [Brussels South Charleroi Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_South_Charleroi_Airport)), and boat transport is available via the [Port of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Brussels). Bicycle-sharing and car-sharing public systems are also available.

The complexity of the Belgian political landscape makes some transportation issues difficult to solve. The Brussels-Capital Region is surrounded by the [Flemish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Flemish_Region) and [Walloon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallonia) regions, which means that the airports, as well as many roads serving Brussels (most notably the [Brussels Ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Ring)), are located in the other two Belgian regions. The city is relatively car-dependent by northern European standards and is considered to be the most congested city in the world according to the [INRIX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INRIX) traffic survey.[^cite_note-auto-328]

### Air



[Brussels Airlines](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Airlines) [Airbus](26220236.md) A319 landing at [Brussels Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Airport) in [Zaventem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaventem)

The Brussels-Capital Region is served by two airports, both located outside of the region's administrative territory. The most notable is [Brussels Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Airport), located in the nearby Flemish municipality of [Zaventem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zaventem), 12 km (10 mi) east of the capital, which can be accessed by motorway (A201), train, taxi and bus. Brussels National Airport has its own railway station. This station is located in the underground (level -1) of the airport terminal building itself. The secondary airport is [Brussels South Charleroi Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_South_Charleroi_Airport), located in [Gosselies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gosselies), a part of the city of [Charleroi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Charleroi) ([Wallonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallonia)), some 50 km (30 mi) south-west of Brussels, which can be accessed by motorway (E19 then E420) or a private bus. There is also [Melsbroek Air Base](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Melsbroek_Air_Base), located in [Steenokkerzeel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Steenokkerzeel), a military airport that shares its infrastructure with Brussels Airport. The aforementioned airports are also the main airports of Belgium.[^cite_note-329]

### Water



View along the [Brussels–Charleroi Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels%E2%80%93Charleroi_Canal) in [Anderlecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anderlecht)

Since the 16th century, Brussels has had its own harbour, the [Port of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Port_of_Brussels). It has been enlarged throughout the centuries to become the second Belgian inland port. Historically situated near the [Place Sainte-Catherine/Sint-Katelijneplein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_Sainte-Catherine), it lies today to the north-west of the region, on the [Brussels–Scheldt Maritime Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels%E2%80%93Scheldt_Maritime_Canal) (commonly called Willebroek Canal), which connects Brussels to [Antwerp](32149462.md) via the [Scheldt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Scheldt). Ships and large barges up to 4,500 t (9,900,000 lb) can penetrate deep into the country, avoiding break-ups and load transfers between Antwerp and the centre of Brussels, hence reducing the cost for companies using the canal, and thus offering a competitive advantage.

Moreover, the connection of the Willebroek Canal with the [Brussels–Charleroi Canal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels%E2%80%93Charleroi_Canal), in the very heart of the capital, creates a north–south link, by means of waterways, between the Netherlands, Flanders and the industrial zone of [Hainaut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hainaut_\(province\)) ([Wallonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallonia)). There, navigation can access the network of French canals, thanks to the important [inclined plane](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canal_inclined_plane) of [Ronquières](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ronqui%C3%A8res_inclined_plane) and the lifts of [Strépy-Bracquegnies](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Str%C3%A9py-Bracquegnies).

The importance of river traffic in Brussels makes it possible to avoid the road equivalent of 740,000 trucks per year—almost 2,000 per day—which, in addition to easing traffic problems, represents an estimated carbon dioxide saving of 51,545 tonnes (113,637,000 lb) per year.[^cite_note-330]

### Train

Further information: [List of railway stations in Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_railway_stations_in_Belgium)



Main hall of [Brussels-South railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels-South_railway_station), home to the [Eurostar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurostar) train service to London

The Brussels-Capital Region has three main train stations: [Brussels-South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels-South_railway_station), [Brussels-Central](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels-Central) and [Brussels-North](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels-North_railway_station), which are also the busiest of the country.[^cite_note-\:2-41] Brussels-South is also served by direct high-speed rail links: to London by [Eurostar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurostar) trains via the [Channel Tunnel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Channel_Tunnel); to [Amsterdam](844.md)[^cite_note-331] by Eurostar "Red" (previously [Thalys](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Thalys)) and *[InterCity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/InterCity)* connections; to Amsterdam, Paris, and [Cologne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cologne) by Eurostar; and to Cologne and [Frankfurt](10992.md) by the German [ICE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Intercity_Express).

The train rails in Brussels go underground, near the centre, through the [North–South connection](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/North%E2%80%93South_connection), with Brussels Central Station also being largely underground. The tunnel itself is only six tracks wide at its narrowest point, which often causes congestion and delays due to heavy use of the route.

The [City of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Brussels) has minor railway stations at [Bockstael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bockstael_railway_station), [Brussels-Chapel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels-Chapel_railway_station), [Brussels-Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels-Congress_railway_station), [Brussels-Luxembourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels-Luxembourg_railway_station), [Brussels-Schuman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schuman_station), [Brussels-West](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels-West_station), [Haren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haren_railway_station_\(Brussels\)), [Haren-South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Haren-South_railway_station) and [Simonis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Simonis_metro_station). In the Brussels Region, there are also railways stations at [Berchem-Sainte-Agathe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berchem-Sainte-Agathe_railway_station), [Boitsfort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boitsfort_railway_station), [Boondael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Boondael_railway_station), [Bordet (Evere)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bordet_railway_station), [Etterbeek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etterbeek_railway_station), [Evere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evere_railway_station), [Forest-East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest-East_railway_station), [Forest-South](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest-South_railway_station), [Jette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jette_railway_station), [Meiser (Schaerbeek)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Meiser_railway_station), [Moensberg (Uccle)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moensberg_railway_station), [Saint-Job (Uccle)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Job_railway_station), [Schaarbeek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schaerbeek_railway_station), [Uccle-Calevoet](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uccle-Calevoet_railway_station), [Uccle-Stalle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uccle-Stalle_railway_station), Vivier d'Oie-Diesdelle (Uccle), [Merode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Merode_station) and [Watermael](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watermael_railway_station).

### Public transport

The [Brussels Intercommunal Transport Company](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Intercommunal_Transport_Company) (STIB/MIVB) is the local [public transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_transport) operator in Brussels. It covers the 19 municipalities of the Brussels-Capital Region and some surface routes extend to the near suburbs in the other two regions, linking with the [De Lijn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_Lijn) network in [Flanders](10878.md) and the [TEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TEC_\(transport\)) network in [Wallonia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallonia).

#### Metro, trams and buses



[Brussels Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Metro) carriage at [Erasme/Erasmus metro station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Erasme/Erasmus_metro_station)

The [Brussels Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Metro) dates back to 1976,[^cite_note-332] but underground lines known as the *[premetro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_premetro)* have been serviced by tramways since 1968. It is the only [rapid transit](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rapid_transit) system in Belgium ([Antwerp](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Antwerp_Pre-metro) and [Charleroi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M%C3%A9tro_L%C3%A9ger_de_Charleroi) both having [light rail](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Light_rail) systems). The network consists of four conventional metro lines and three *premetro* lines. The metro-grade lines are [M1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Metro_line_1), [M2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Metro_line_2), [M5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Metro_line_5), and [M6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Metro_line_6), with some shared sections, covering a total of 40 km (25 mi).[^cite_note-The_network_and_vehicles-333] As of 2017, the Metro network within the region has a total of [69 metro and *premetro* stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Brussels_Metro_stations). The Metro is an important [means of transport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transport_in_Brussels), connecting with six [railway stations](26161590.md) of the [National Railway Company of Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Railway_Company_of_Belgium) (SNCB/NMBS), and many tram and bus stops operated by STIB/MIVB, as well as with [Flemish](10878.md) [De Lijn](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/De_Lijn) and [Walloon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wallonia) [TEC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/TEC_\(transport\)) bus stops.

A comprehensive [bus](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_buses) and [tram network](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_trams) covers the region. As of 2017, the Brussels tram system consists of 17 tram lines (three of which – lines [T4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_tram_route_4), [T7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_tram_route_7) and T10 – qualify as *premetro* lines that partly travel over underground sections that were intended to be eventually converted into metro lines).[^cite_note-newscientist-334] The total route length is 139 km (86 mi),[^cite_note-The_network_and_vehicles-333] making it one of the largest tram networks in Europe. The Brussels bus network is complementary to the rail network. It consists of 50 bus routes and 11 night routes, spanning 445 km (277 mi).[^cite_note-The_network_and_vehicles-333]

Since April 2007, STIB/MIVB has also been operating a night bus network called Noctis on Friday and Saturday nights from midnight until 3 a.m.[^cite_note-noctisbrus-335] The service consists of 11 routes (N04, N05, N06, N08, N09, N10, N11, N12, N13, N16 and N18).[^cite_note-336] The fare on these night buses is the same as during the day. All the lines leave from the [Place de la Bourse/Beursplein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Place_de_la_Bourse,_Brussels) in the city centre at 30 minutes intervals and cover all the main streets in the capital, as they radiate outwards to the suburbs.[^cite_note-337] Noctis services returned from 2 July 2021 after over a year of disruption due to the [COVID-19 pandemic in Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Belgium).[^cite_note-noctisbrus-335]

#### Other public transport



[Villo!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Villo!) [shared bicycles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shared_bicycle) in Brussels

Since 2003, Brussels has had a car-sharing service operated by the [Bremen](9420388.md) company Cambio, in partnership with STIB/MIVB and the local ridesharing company Taxi Stop.[^cite_note-338] In 2006, a [public bicycle-sharing programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Community_bicycle_program) was introduced. The scheme was subsequently taken over by [Villo!](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Villo!). Since 2008, this night-time public transport service has been supplemented by Collecto, a shared taxi system, which operates on weekdays between 11 p.m. and 6 a.m.[^cite_note-339] In 2012, the Zen Car electric car-sharing scheme was launched in the university and European areas, though it ceased operating in the city in 2020.[^cite_note-340]

### Road network



The [Rue de la Loi/Wetstraat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rue_de_la_Loi) is one of the city's main streets.

In medieval times, Brussels stood at the intersection of routes running north–south (the modern Rue Haute/Hoogstraat) and east–west (Chaussée de Gand/Gentsesteenweg–Rue du Marché aux Herbes/Grasmarkt–Rue de Namur/Naamsestraat). The ancient pattern of streets, radiating from the [Grand-Place](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand-Place), in large part remains, but has been overlaid by boulevards built [over the river Senne](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Covering_of_the_Senne), [over the city walls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fortifications_of_Brussels#Construction_of_the_Small_Ring) and over the railway connection between the North and South Stations. In 2012, Brussels had the most congested traffic in Europe and North America, according to US traffic information platform [INRIX](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INRIX).[^cite_note-341]

Brussels is the hub of a range of national roads, the main ones being clockwise: the [N1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N1_road_\(Belgium\)) (N to [Breda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Breda)), [N2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N2_road_\(Belgium\)) (E to [Maastricht](20125.md)), [N3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N3_road_\(Belgium\)) (E to [Aachen](1520.md)), [N4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N4_road_\(Belgium\)) (SE to [Luxembourg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Luxembourg_City)), [N5](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N5_road_\(Belgium\)) (S to [Reims](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Reims)), N6 (S to [Maubeuge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maubeuge)), N7 (SW to [Lille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lille)), [N8](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/N8_road_\(Belgium\)) (W to [Koksijde](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koksijde)) and N9 (NW to [Ostend](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ostend)).[^cite_note-342] Usually named *chaussées*/*steenwegen*, these roads normally run in a straight line, but sometimes lose themselves in a maze of narrow shopping streets. The region is skirted by the [European route E19](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_route_E19) (N-S) and the [E40](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_route_E40) (E-W), while the [E411](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/E411) leads away to the SE. Brussels has an [orbital motorway](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Orbital_motorway), numbered R0 (R-zero) and commonly referred to as the [Ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Ring). It is pear-shaped, as the southern side was never built as originally conceived, owing to residents' objections.

The city centre, sometimes known as the [Pentagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentagon_\(Brussels\)), is surrounded by an inner ring road, the [Small Ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Small_Ring,_Brussels) (French: *Petite Ceinture*, Dutch: *Kleine Ring*), a sequence of boulevards formally numbered R20. These were built upon the site of the [second set of city walls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_walls_of_Brussels) following their demolition. The [Metro line 2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Metro_line_2) runs alongside (not actually under) much of these. Since June 2015, a number of [central boulevards](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Central_Boulevards_of_Brussels) inside the Pentagon have become car-free, limiting transit traffic through the old city.[^cite_note-343]

On the eastern side of the region, the R21 or [Greater Ring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Greater_ring_\(Brussels\)) (French: *Grande Ceinture*, Dutch: *Grote Ring*) is formed by a string of boulevards that curves round from [Laeken](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Laeken) to [Uccle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uccle). Some *premetro* stations (see [Brussels Metro](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Brussels_Metro)) were built on that route. A little further out, a stretch numbered R22 leads from Zaventem to [Saint-Job](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Job_railway_station).

## Security and emergency services

### Police

Further information: [Law enforcement in Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Law_enforcement_in_Belgium)



Policeman in Brussels

The Brussels local police, supported by the federal police, is responsible for law enforcement in Brussels. The 19 municipalities of the Brussels-Capital Region are divided into six police zones,[^cite_note-344] all bilingual (French and Dutch):

- 5339 Brussels Capital Ixelles: the [City of Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/City_of_Brussels) and [Ixelles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ixelles)

- 5340 Brussels West: [Berchem-Sainte-Agathe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Berchem-Sainte-Agathe), [Ganshoren](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ganshoren), [Jette](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jette), [Koekelberg](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Koekelberg) and [Molenbeek-Saint-Jean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Molenbeek-Saint-Jean)

- 5341 South: [Anderlecht](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anderlecht), [Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Forest,_Belgium) and [Saint-Gilles](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Gilles,_Belgium)

- 5342 Uccle/Watermael-Boitsfort/Auderghem: [Auderghem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Auderghem), [Uccle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uccle) and [Watermael-Boitsfort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Watermael-Boitsfort)

- 5343 Montgomery: [Etterbeek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Etterbeek), [Woluwe-Saint-Lambert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woluwe-Saint-Lambert) and [Woluwe-Saint-Pierre](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Woluwe-Saint-Pierre)

- 5344 Polbruno: [Evere](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Evere), [Saint-Josse-ten-Noode](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saint-Josse-ten-Noode) and [Schaerbeek](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Schaerbeek)

### Fire department

The Brussels Fire and Emergency Medical Care Service, commonly known by its acronym SIAMU (DBDMH), operates in the 19 municipalities of Brussels.[^cite_note-345] It is a class X fire department and the largest [fire service](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_service) in Belgium in terms of annual operations, equipment, and personnel. It has 9 [fire stations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fire_station), spread over the entire Brussels-Capital Region, and employs about 1,000 professional [firefighters](146303.md). As well as preventing and fighting fires, SIAMU also provides emergency medical care services in Brussels via its centralised 100 number (and the single 112 emergency number for the 27 countries of the European Union). It is bilingual (French–Dutch).

## Parks and green spaces

Main article: [List of parks and gardens in Brussels](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_parks_and_gardens_in_Brussels)

Brussels is one of the greenest capitals in Europe, with over 8,000 hectares of green spaces.[^cite_note-346] Vegetation cover and natural areas are higher in the outskirts, where they have limited the [peri-urbanisation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peri-urbanisation) of the capital, but they decrease sharply towards the centre of Brussels; 10% in the central [Pentagon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pentagon_\(Brussels\)), 30% of the municipalities in the first ring, and 71% of the municipalities in the second ring are occupied by green spaces.

Many parks and gardens, both public and privately owned, are scattered throughout the city. In addition to this, the [Sonian Forest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sonian_Forest) is located in its southern part and stretches out over the three [Belgian regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communities,_regions_and_language_areas_of_Belgium#Regions). As of 2017, it has been inscribed as a [UNESCO World Heritage Site](44940.md), the only Belgian component to the multinational inscription '[Primeval Beech Forests of the Carpathians and Other Regions of Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Primeval_Beech_Forests_of_the_Carpathians_and_Other_Regions_of_Europe)'.[^cite_note-347]

## Partner cities and regions

See also: [List of twin towns and sister cities in Belgium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_twin_towns_and_sister_cities_in_Belgium)

Several of the municipalities within the Brussels-Capital Region have symbolic [twin city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sister_city) agreements with municipalities elsewhere, but the Brussels Region itself has none. Besides cooperation with many other cities and regions through such institutions as the [Assembly of European Regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Assembly_of_European_Regions) and the [European Forum for Urban Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/European_Forum_for_Urban_Security), Brussels has direct bilateral cooperation or development agreements with the following cities and regions:[^cite_note-348]

- [Aichi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aichi)

- [Berlin](3354.md)

- [Budapest](36787.md)

- [Havana](49719.md)

- [Île-de-France](1716427.md)

- [Kinshasa](45773.md)

- [Kyiv](585629.md)

- [Lille](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lille)

- [Luxembourg](17515.md)

- [Quebec](7954867.md)

- [Rabat-Salé-Kénitra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rabat-Sal%C3%A9-K%C3%A9nitra)

- [Ramallah and al-Bireh Governorate](12007076.md)

- [Seoul](19159283.md)

  [^cite_note-349]

- [Tokyo](30057.md)

Article 4: Belgium comprises four linguistic regions: the Dutch-speaking region, the French-speaking region, the bilingual region of Brussels-Capital and the German-speaking region.
